From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF88, R41.0, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, um… it’s probably not a huge thing, but my wife has noticed it, and I guess I have too. She’ll be telling me something—like about my cousin’s appointments or rides—and I’m looking right at her, so I feel like I should be hearing it, but then a minute later I realize I didn’t really take in half of what she said. It’s a little embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you weren't paying attention to what your wife was saying, or you were paying attention but just couldn't remember what she said later?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’m not really taking it in at the time, even though it looks like I am. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but lately I’ve forgotten things I meant to do—like returning a call from the doctor’s office, or picking up a prescription. And a couple times I’ve cut it closer than I should leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments. It’s not all the time, but enough that I’ve noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty with remembering things, like conversations and tasks, caused any problems at work or with friends?\n\nPatient: Not work, really—I’m not working right now. With friends, not so much that I’ve noticed. It’s more at home, I guess. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t totally count on me with my cousin’s care, and I understand why, even if I don’t like admitting that.\n\nI do some catering-type tasks too, and I’ve had to double-check myself or redo little things so I don’t mix up small jobs. It hasn’t been a disaster or anything, but… yeah, it’s caused some tension. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've forgotten something?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I wouldn’t say I get really worked up every time, but I do feel bad when I realize I dropped the ball. Mostly embarrassed, I guess—especially if my wife already reminded me. Then I’m kind of irritated with myself, like, “Why didn’t I just remember that?” But I try not to make a big thing of it.\n\nDoctor: Has anything particularly stressful or upsetting happened to you recently or in the past that might be related to when you started noticing these memory issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was anything, like, traumatic exactly. The main change was my cousin moving in with us after surgery… and that’s been about 11 months now. Since then, I’ve noticed the remembering stuff being harder. I guess it could be related to the extra responsibility, but I don’t want to overstate it. Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or sad about your situation, beyond just feeling embarrassed or irritated when you forget things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. And a couple times I’ve cut it closer than I should leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments. It’s not all the time, but enough that I’ve noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty with remembering things, like conversations and tasks, caused any problems at work or with friends?\n\nPatient: Not work, really—I’m not working right now. With friends, not so much that I’ve noticed. It’s more at home, I guess. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t totally count on me with my cousin’s care, and I understand why, even if I don’t like admitting that.\n\nI do some catering-type tasks too, and I’ve had to double-check myself or redo little things so I don’t mix up small jobs. It hasn’t been a disaster or anything, but… yeah, it’s caused some tension. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've forgotten something?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I wouldn’t say I get really worked up every time, but I do feel bad when I realize I dropped the ball. Mostly embarrassed, I guess—especially if my wife already reminded me. Then I’m kind of irritated with myself, like, “Why didn’t I just remember that?” But I try not to make a big thing of it.\n\nDoctor: Has anything particularly stressful or upsetting happened to you recently or in the past that might be related to when you started noticing these memory issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was anything, like, traumatic exactly. The main change was my cousin moving in with us after surgery… and that’s been about 11 months now. Since then, I’ve noticed the remembering stuff being harder. I guess it could be related to the extra responsibility, but I don’t want to overstate it. Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or sad about your situation, beyond just feeling embarrassed or irritated when you forget things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I get discouraged sometimes, sure, but I wouldn’t say I’m overwhelmed or really sad about it. It’s more like… I wish I handled things better, and I don’t like disappointing my wife. But I’m managing, I think. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble learning new things lately?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, I’m probably not picking up brand-new things every day like I used to, but when something comes up, I think I can learn it okay. Maybe I need it explained clearly or I might ask again, but I wouldn’t say it’s been a big problem. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I mean, I can start something okay, but if I’m trying to work out a catering order or sit through a parish planning call, after a while my mind just sort of drifts. Then I realize I didn’t really take in what I was reading or hearing, and I have to go back over the same notes again. It’s not constant, but it does happen enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have off days like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I feel sad or empty most of the day. That sounds stronger than what I experience, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

The main change was my cousin moving in with us after surgery… and that’s been about 11 months now. Since then, I’ve noticed the remembering stuff being harder. I guess it could be related to the extra responsibility, but I don’t want to overstate it. Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or sad about your situation, beyond just feeling embarrassed or irritated when you forget things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I get discouraged sometimes, sure, but I wouldn’t say I’m overwhelmed or really sad about it. It’s more like… I wish I handled things better, and I don’t like disappointing my wife. But I’m managing, I think. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble learning new things lately?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, I’m probably not picking up brand-new things every day like I used to, but when something comes up, I think I can learn it okay. Maybe I need it explained clearly or I might ask again, but I wouldn’t say it’s been a big problem. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I mean, I can start something okay, but if I’m trying to work out a catering order or sit through a parish planning call, after a while my mind just sort of drifts. Then I realize I didn’t really take in what I was reading or hearing, and I have to go back over the same notes again. It’s not constant, but it does happen enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have off days like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I feel sad or empty most of the day. That sounds stronger than what I experience, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy. Maybe I don’t always get around to them as much, but the interest is still there, if that’s what you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, um… it’s probably not a huge thing, but my wife has noticed it, and I guess I have too. She’ll be telling me something—like about my cousin’s appointments or rides—and I’m looking right at her, so I feel like I should be hearing it, but then a minute later I realize I didn’t really take in half of what she said. It’s a little embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you weren't paying attention to what your wife was saying, or you were paying attention but just couldn't remember what she said later?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’m not really taking it in at the time, even though it looks like I am. Like, my wife will be telling me about my cousin’s appointments or who needs a ride, and I’m looking right at her, nodding along, but then a minute later I realize I only caught bits and pieces. I don’t mean to ignore her or anything—it just sort of doesn’t land, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering other things in your daily life, aside from conversations with your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but lately I’ve forgotten things I meant to do—like returning a call from the doctor’s office, or picking up a prescription. And a couple times I’ve cut it closer than I should leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments. It’s not all the time, but enough that I’ve noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty with remembering things, like conversations and tasks, caused any problems at work or with friends?\n\nPatient: Not work, really—I’m not working right now. With friends, not so much that I’ve noticed. It’s more at home, I guess. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t totally count on me with my cousin’s care, and I understand why, even if I don’t like admitting that.\n\nI do some catering-type tasks too, and I’ve had to double-check myself or redo little things so I don’t mix up small jobs. It hasn’t been a disaster or anything, but… yeah, it’s caused some tension. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've forgotten something?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I wouldn’t say I get really worked up every time, but I do feel bad when I realize I dropped the ball. Mostly embarrassed, I guess—especially if my wife already reminded me. Then I’m kind of irritated with myself, like, “Why didn’t I just remember that?” But I try not to make a big thing of it.\n\nDoctor: Has anything particularly stressful or upsetting happened to you recently or in the past that might be related to when you started noticing these memory issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was anything, like, traumatic exactly. I guess it could be related to the extra responsibility, but I don’t want to overstate it. Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or sad about your situation, beyond just feeling embarrassed or irritated when you forget things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. But I’m managing, I think. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble learning new things lately?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I mean, I can start something okay, but if I’m trying to work out a catering order or sit through a parish planning call, after a while my mind just sort of drifts. She’ll be telling me something—like about my cousin’s appointments or rides—and I’m looking right at her, so I feel like I should be hearing it, but then a minute later I realize I didn’t really take in half of what she said. It’s a little embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you weren't paying attention to what your wife was saying, or you were paying attention but just couldn't remember what she said later?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’m not really taking it in at the time, even though it looks like I am. Like, my wife will be telling me about my cousin’s appointments or who needs a ride, and I’m looking right at her, nodding along, but then a minute later I realize I only caught bits and pieces. I don’t mean to ignore her or anything—it just sort of doesn’t land, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering other things in your daily life, aside from conversations with your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but lately I’ve forgotten things I meant to do—like returning a call from the doctor’s office, or picking up a prescription. And a couple times I’ve cut it closer than I should leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments. It’s not all the time, but enough that I’ve noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty with remembering things, like conversations and tasks, caused any problems at work or with friends?\n\nPatient: Not work, really—I’m not working right now. With friends, not so much that I’ve noticed. It’s more at home, I guess. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t totally count on me with my cousin’s care, and I understand why, even if I don’t like admitting that.\n\nI do some catering-type tasks too, and I’ve had to double-check myself or redo little things so I don’t mix up small jobs. It hasn’t been a disaster or anything, but… yeah, it’s caused some tension. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've forgotten something?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I wouldn’t say I get really worked up every time, but I do feel bad when I realize I dropped the ball. Mostly embarrassed, I guess—especially if my wife already reminded me. Then I’m kind of irritated with myself, like, “Why didn’t I just remember that?” But I try not to make a big thing of it.\n\nDoctor: Has anything particularly stressful or upsetting happened to you recently or in the past that might be related to when you started noticing these memory issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was anything, like, traumatic exactly. The main change was my cousin moving in with us after surgery… and that’s been about 11 months now. Since then, I’ve noticed the remembering stuff being harder. I guess it could be related to the extra responsibility, but I don’t want to overstate it. Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or sad about your situation, beyond just feeling embarrassed or irritated when you forget things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I get discouraged sometimes, sure, but I wouldn’t say I’m overwhelmed or really sad about it. It’s more like… I wish I handled things better, and I don’t like disappointing my wife. But I’m managing, I think. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble learning new things lately?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, I’m probably not picking up brand-new things every day like I used to, but when something comes up, I think I can learn it okay. Maybe I need it explained clearly or I might ask again, but I wouldn’t say it’s been a big problem. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I mean, I can start something okay, but if I’m trying to work out a catering order or sit through a parish planning call, after a while my mind just sort of drifts. Then I realize I didn’t really take in what I was reading or hearing, and I have to go back over the same notes again. It’s not constant, but it does happen enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have off days like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I feel sad or empty most of the day. That sounds stronger than what I experience, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy. Maybe I don’t always get around to them as much, but the interest is still there, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll feel bad if I forget something or let something slip, like anybody would, but I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling guilty over things that shouldn’t bother me. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been experiencing a lowered mood more often than not?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I can get frustrated or a little down here and there, but I wouldn’t say my mood is lowered more often than not. At least that doesn’t sound like me, if I’m understanding it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been taking less pleasure in activities that you normally find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I still get pleasure from the things I like when I actually do them. I guess I might not always make time for them, but it’s not that they don’t feel enjoyable anymore, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but lately I’ve forgotten things I meant to do—like returning a call from the doctor’s office, or picking up a prescription. And a couple times I’ve cut it closer than I should leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments. It’s not all the time, but enough that I’ve noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty with remembering things, like conversations and tasks, caused any problems at work or with friends?\n\nPatient: Not work, really—I’m not working right now. With friends, not so much that I’ve noticed. It’s more at home, I guess. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t totally count on me with my cousin’s care, and I understand why, even if I don’t like admitting that.\n\nI do some catering-type tasks too, and I’ve had to double-check myself or redo little things so I don’t mix up small jobs. It hasn’t been a disaster or anything, but… yeah, it’s caused some tension. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've forgotten something?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I wouldn’t say I get really worked up every time, but I do feel bad when I realize I dropped the ball. Mostly embarrassed, I guess—especially if my wife already reminded me. Then I’m kind of irritated with myself, like, “Why didn’t I just remember that?” But I try not to make a big thing of it.\n\nDoctor: Has anything particularly stressful or upsetting happened to you recently or in the past that might be related to when you started noticing these memory issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was anything, like, traumatic exactly. The main change was my cousin moving in with us after surgery… and that’s been about 11 months now. Since then, I’ve noticed the remembering stuff being harder. I guess it could be related to the extra responsibility, but I don’t want to overstate it. Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or sad about your situation, beyond just feeling embarrassed or irritated when you forget things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I get discouraged sometimes, sure, but I wouldn’t say I’m overwhelmed or really sad about it. It’s more like… I wish I handled things better, and I don’t like disappointing my wife. But I’m managing, I think. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble learning new things lately?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, I’m probably not picking up brand-new things every day like I used to, but when something comes up, I think I can learn it okay. Maybe I need it explained clearly or I might ask again, but I wouldn’t say it’s been a big problem. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I mean, I can start something okay, but if I’m trying to work out a catering order or sit through a parish planning call, after a while my mind just sort of drifts. Then I realize I didn’t really take in what I was reading or hearing, and I have to go back over the same notes again. It’s not constant, but it does happen enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have off days like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I feel sad or empty most of the day. That sounds stronger than what I experience, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy. Maybe I don’t always get around to them as much, but the interest is still there, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll feel bad if I forget something or let something slip, like anybody would, but I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling guilty over things that shouldn’t bother me. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been experiencing a lowered mood more often than not?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I can get frustrated or a little down here and there, but I wouldn’t say my mood is lowered more often than not. At least that doesn’t sound like me, if I’m understanding it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been taking less pleasure in activities that you normally find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I still get pleasure from the things I like when I actually do them. I guess I might not always make time for them, but it’s not that they don’t feel enjoyable anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're struggling to cope with your daily life due to these memory issues and other stressors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart or anything, but… yeah, it’s been getting in the way some. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t fully count on me with helping care for my cousin, and I understand why, even if I don’t like admitting it.\n\nAnd with the little catering jobs, I’ve had to double-check myself more, or sometimes redo things, just so I don’t mix up small details. So I’m coping, I think, but it’s definitely causing some tension and extra work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d call it worthlessness. I mean, I feel bad when I drop the ball or someone’s disappointed in me, sure… but I don’t sit around thinking I’m worthless or anything like that. At least, I don’t think so.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "determine presence of significant depressive symptoms", "question": "Have you been having thoughts that life is not worth living?"}

It’s not constant, but it does happen enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have off days like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I feel sad or empty most of the day. That sounds stronger than what I experience, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy. Maybe I don’t always get around to them as much, but the interest is still there, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll feel bad if I forget something or let something slip, like anybody would, but I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling guilty over things that shouldn’t bother me. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been experiencing a lowered mood more often than not?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I can get frustrated or a little down here and there, but I wouldn’t say my mood is lowered more often than not. At least that doesn’t sound like me, if I’m understanding it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been taking less pleasure in activities that you normally find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I still get pleasure from the things I like when I actually do them. I guess I might not always make time for them, but it’s not that they don’t feel enjoyable anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're struggling to cope with your daily life due to these memory issues and other stressors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart or anything, but… yeah, it’s been getting in the way some. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t fully count on me with helping care for my cousin, and I understand why, even if I don’t like admitting it.\n\nAnd with the little catering jobs, I’ve had to double-check myself more, or sometimes redo things, just so I don’t mix up small details. So I’m coping, I think, but it’s definitely causing some tension and extra work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d call it worthlessness. I mean, I feel bad when I drop the ball or someone’s disappointed in me, sure… but I don’t sit around thinking I’m worthless or anything like that. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: No… no, I wouldn’t say that. I mean, I get frustrated with myself sometimes, but I haven’t been thinking that life isn’t worth living or anything like that. That sounds right to say, I think.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, um… it’s probably not a huge thing, but my wife has noticed it, and I guess I have too. She’ll be telling me something—like about my cousin’s appointments or rides—and I’m looking right at her, so I feel like I should be hearing it, but then a minute later I realize I didn’t really take in half of what she said. It’s a little embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you weren't paying attention to what your wife was saying, or you were paying attention but just couldn't remember what she said later?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’m not really taking it in at the time, even though it looks like I am. Like, my wife will be telling me about my cousin’s appointments or who needs a ride, and I’m looking right at her, nodding along, but then a minute later I realize I only caught bits and pieces. I don’t mean to ignore her or anything—it just sort of doesn’t land, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering other things in your daily life, aside from conversations with your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but lately I’ve forgotten things I meant to do—like returning a call from the doctor’s office, or picking up a prescription. And a couple times I’ve cut it closer than I should leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments. It’s not all the time, but enough that I’ve noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty with remembering things, like conversations and tasks, caused any problems at work or with friends?\n\nPatient: Not work, really—I’m not working right now. With friends, not so much that I’ve noticed. It’s more at home, I guess. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t totally count on me with my cousin’s care, and I understand why, even if I don’t like admitting that.\n\nI do some catering-type tasks too, and I’ve had to double-check myself or redo little things so I don’t mix up small jobs. It hasn’t been a disaster or anything, but… yeah, it’s caused some tension. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've forgotten something?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I wouldn’t say I get really worked up every time, but I do feel bad when I realize I dropped the ball. Mostly embarrassed, I guess—especially if my wife already reminded me. Then I’m kind of irritated with myself, like, “Why didn’t I just remember that?” But I try not to make a big thing of it.\n\nDoctor: Has anything particularly stressful or upsetting happened to you recently or in the past that might be related to when you started noticing these memory issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was anything, like, traumatic exactly. The main change was my cousin moving in with us after surgery… and that’s been about 11 months now. Since then, I’ve noticed the remembering stuff being harder. I guess it could be related to the extra responsibility, but I don’t want to overstate it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, um… it’s probably not a huge thing, but my wife has noticed it, and I guess I have too. She’ll be telling me something—like about my cousin’s appointments or rides—and I’m looking right at her, so I feel like I should be hearing it, but then a minute later I realize I didn’t really take in half of what she said. It’s a little embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you weren't paying attention to what your wife was saying, or you were paying attention but just couldn't remember what she said later?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’m not really taking it in at the time, even though it looks like I am. Like, my wife will be telling me about my cousin’s appointments or who needs a ride, and I’m looking right at her, nodding along, but then a minute later I realize I only caught bits and pieces. I don’t mean to ignore her or anything—it just sort of doesn’t land, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering other things in your daily life, aside from conversations with your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but lately I’ve forgotten things I meant to do—like returning a call from the doctor’s office, or picking up a prescription. And a couple times I’ve cut it closer than I should leaving the house for one of my cousin’s appointments. It’s not all the time, but enough that I’ve noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty with remembering things, like conversations and tasks, caused any problems at work or with friends?\n\nPatient: Not work, really—I’m not working right now. With friends, not so much that I’ve noticed. It’s more at home, I guess. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t totally count on me with my cousin’s care, and I understand why, even if I don’t like admitting that.\n\nI do some catering-type tasks too, and I’ve had to double-check myself or redo little things so I don’t mix up small jobs. It hasn’t been a disaster or anything, but… yeah, it’s caused some tension. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've forgotten something?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I wouldn’t say I get really worked up every time, but I do feel bad when I realize I dropped the ball. Mostly embarrassed, I guess—especially if my wife already reminded me. Then I’m kind of irritated with myself, like, “Why didn’t I just remember that?” But I try not to make a big thing of it.\n\nDoctor: Has anything particularly stressful or upsetting happened to you recently or in the past that might be related to when you started noticing these memory issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was anything, like, traumatic exactly. The main change was my cousin moving in with us after surgery… and that’s been about 11 months now. Since then, I’ve noticed the remembering stuff being harder. I guess it could be related to the extra responsibility, but I don’t want to overstate it. Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or sad about your situation, beyond just feeling embarrassed or irritated when you forget things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I get discouraged sometimes, sure, but I wouldn’t say I’m overwhelmed or really sad about it. It’s more like… I wish I handled things better, and I don’t like disappointing my wife. But I’m managing, I think. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble learning new things lately?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, I’m probably not picking up brand-new things every day like I used to, but when something comes up, I think I can learn it okay. Maybe I need it explained clearly or I might ask again, but I wouldn’t say it’s been a big problem. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I mean, I can start something okay, but if I’m trying to work out a catering order or sit through a parish planning call, after a while my mind just sort of drifts. Then I realize I didn’t really take in what I was reading or hearing, and I have to go back over the same notes again. It’s not constant, but it does happen enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have off days like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I feel sad or empty most of the day. That sounds stronger than what I experience, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy. Maybe I don’t always get around to them as much, but the interest is still there, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll feel bad if I forget something or let something slip, like anybody would, but I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling guilty over things that shouldn’t bother me. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been experiencing a lowered mood more often than not?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I can get frustrated or a little down here and there, but I wouldn’t say my mood is lowered more often than not. At least that doesn’t sound like me, if I’m understanding it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been taking less pleasure in activities that you normally find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I still get pleasure from the things I like when I actually do them. I guess I might not always make time for them, but it’s not that they don’t feel enjoyable anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're struggling to cope with your daily life due to these memory issues and other stressors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart or anything, but… yeah, it’s been getting in the way some. So I’m coping, I think, but it’s definitely causing some tension and extra work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d call it worthlessness. I mean, I feel bad when I drop the ball or someone’s disappointed in me, sure… but I don’t sit around thinking I’m worthless or anything like that. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but lately I’ve forgotten things I meant to do—like returning a call from the doctor’s office, or picking up a prescription. It hasn’t been a disaster or anything, but… yeah, it’s caused some tension. Then I’m kind of irritated with myself, like, “Why didn’t I just remember that?” But I try not to make a big thing of it.\n\nDoctor: Has anything particularly stressful or upsetting happened to you recently or in the past that might be related to when you started noticing these memory issues?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was anything, like, traumatic exactly. I guess it could be related to the extra responsibility, but I don’t want to overstate it. Does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or sad about your situation, beyond just feeling embarrassed or irritated when you forget things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I’m probably not picking up brand-new things every day like I used to, but when something comes up, I think I can learn it okay. Maybe I need it explained clearly or I might ask again, but I wouldn’t say it’s been a big problem. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I mean, I can start something okay, but if I’m trying to work out a catering order or sit through a parish planning call, after a while my mind just sort of drifts. Then I realize I didn’t really take in what I was reading or hearing, and I have to go back over the same notes again. It’s not constant, but it does happen enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have off days like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I feel sad or empty most of the day. That sounds stronger than what I experience, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I still enjoy the things I usually enjoy. Maybe I don’t always get around to them as much, but the interest is still there, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll feel bad if I forget something or let something slip, like anybody would, but I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling guilty over things that shouldn’t bother me. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been experiencing a lowered mood more often than not?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I can get frustrated or a little down here and there, but I wouldn’t say my mood is lowered more often than not. At least that doesn’t sound like me, if I’m understanding it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been taking less pleasure in activities that you normally find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I still get pleasure from the things I like when I actually do them. I guess I might not always make time for them, but it’s not that they don’t feel enjoyable anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're struggling to cope with your daily life due to these memory issues and other stressors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart or anything, but… yeah, it’s been getting in the way some.
